Theorem 7.37.

If
\mathsf{1SDFE}
is sub-exponentially adaptively single-key secure,
i\mathcal{O}
is a sub-exponentially secure IO for
\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
, and
\mathsf{PPRF}
is a sub-exponentially secure PPRF,
\mathsf{SDFE}
is adaptively secure.

Proof.

We define a sequence of hybrid games.
For the hybrid games, we present some definitions.
Let
q
be the total number of key queries by
\mathpzc{A}
. Note that
q
could be any polynomial.
When we start the adaptive security game, the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is fixed, and
q
is also fixed. We choose tags
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
q
key queries at the beginning of the game.
We can interpret
\ell
bit strings as integers and assume that there is no
i,j
such that
i\neq j
and
\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}
without loss of generality.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}(\mathsf{coin})
:

This is the original adaptive security game.

1.

The challenger generates
(\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\widehat{\mathsf{msk}})=(i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}),\mathsf{K})\leftarrow\mathsf{SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends key queries
f_{k}
to the challenger and the challenger generates
r_{\tau_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau_{k})
and
(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau_{k}},\mathsf{msk}_{\tau_{k}})\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};r_{\tau_{k}})
, and returns
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}_{f_{k}}=(\tau_{k},\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k},\tau_{k}})
where
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k},\tau_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{msk}_{\tau_{k}},f_{k})
.

3.

At some point
\mathpzc{A}
sends a pair
(x_{0},x_{1})
to the challenger.
If
f_{i}(x_{0})=f_{i}(x_{1})
, the challenger generates
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{K}^{\prime},x_{\mathsf{coin}}]
defined in
Figure

3
and sends
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{K}^{\prime},x_{\mathsf{coin}}])
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

Again,
\mathpzc{A}
sends
f_{k}
to the challenger and the challenger returns
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}_{f_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\widehat{\mathsf{msk}},f_{k})
if
f_{k}(x_{0})=f_{k}(x_{1})
.

5.

When
\mathpzc{A}
outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
, the game outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
.

Let
i\in[2^{\ell}]
.
In the following hybrid games, we gradually change
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
for the
i
-th tag
\tau^{\prime}_{i}
, which is the
\ell
-bit string representation of
i\in[2^{\ell}]
.
So, it could happen that
\tau^{\prime}_{i}=\tau_{j}
for some
i\in[2^{\ell}]
and
j\in[q]
.
Let
\mathsf{Adv}_{0}(\mathsf{coin})
and
\mathsf{Adv}_{x}^{i}
be the advantage of
\mathpzc{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}(\mathsf{coin})
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{x}^{i}
, respectively.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i}
:

We generate
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
as obfuscated
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
described in
Figure

4
. In this hybrid game, we set
r_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
,
\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K},\tau^{\prime}_{i})
,
(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},\mathsf{msk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}})\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};r_{i})
, and
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}\leftarrow i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}^{\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime}_{i},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}},\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}])
.

When
i=1
, the behavior of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is the same as that of
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
since the hard-wired
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{1}}
in
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is the same as the output of
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
on the input
\tau^{\prime}_{1}
. Their size is also the same since we pad circuit
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
to have the same size as
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{*}
. Then, we can use the indistinguishability of
i\mathcal{O}_{1}
and it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{0}(0) - \Adv_{1}^{1}}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{i}
:

The challenge ciphertext is generated by obfuscating
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
described in
Figure

5
. In this hybrid game, we set
r^{\prime}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
,
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}_{i})
,
\mathsf{ct}_{i}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{0};r^{\prime}_{i})
,
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
, and
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}},\allowbreak x_{0},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}}])
.

When
i=1
, the behavior of
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is the same as that of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
since the hard-wired
\mathsf{ct}_{1}
in
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is the same as the output of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
on the input
1
. Both circuits have the same size by padding
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}
.

In addition, for
i\geq 2
, the behavior of
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
does not change between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{i}
.
Thus, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{2}^{i}- \Adv_{1}^{i} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
for every
i\in[2^{\ell}]
due to the indistinguishability of
i\mathcal{O}_{2}
.

Setup Circuit
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}},\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}}](\tau)

Hardwired:

tag
\tau^{\prime}
, puncturable PRF key
\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}}
, and
\mathsf{1SDFE}
public-key
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Input:

tag
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

Padding:

circuit is padded to size
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{S}}\coloneqq\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{S}}(\lambda,n,s)
, which is determined in analysis.

1.

If
\tau^{\prime}=\tau
, output
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

2.

Else, compute
r\leftarrow\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}}(\tau)
.

3.

Compute
(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau},\mathsf{msk}_{\tau})\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};r)
and output
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau}
.

Figure 4:
Description of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}},\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}}]
.

Encryption Circuit
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}},x_{0},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}}](\tau)

Hardwired:

tag
\tau^{\prime}
, public key
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
(this is an obfuscated circuit), puncturable PRF key
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}}
, plaintexts
x_{0},x_{1}
, and ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Input:

tag
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

Padding:

circuit is padded to size
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}\coloneqq\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}(\lambda,n,s)
, which is determined in analysis.

1.

If
\tau^{\prime}=\tau
, output
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

2.

Else, compute
r^{\prime}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}}(\tau)
and the circuit
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
on input
\tau
, that is,
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}(\tau)
,

If
\tau>\tau^{\prime}
:

Output
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau},x_{0};r^{\prime}_{\tau})
.

If
\tau<\tau^{\prime}
:

Output
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau},x_{1};r^{\prime}_{\tau})
.

Figure 5:
Description of
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}},x_{0},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i}
:

We change
r_{i}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
and
r^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
into uniformly random
r_{i}
and
r^{\prime}_{i}
. Due to the pseudorandomness at punctured points of puncturable PRF, it holds that
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{3}^{i}- \Adv_{2}^{i} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
for every
i\in[2^{\ell}]
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i}
:

We change
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
from
\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{0})
to
\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{1})
.
In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i}
, we do not need randomness to generate
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
.
We just hardwire
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
into
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
, respectively. Therefore, for every
i\in[2^{\ell}]
,
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{4}^{i}- \Adv_{3}^{i} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
follows from the adaptive security of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
under the master public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
.

Lemma 7.38.

It holds that
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{4}^{i}- \Adv_{3}^{i} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
for all
i\in[2^{\ell}]
if
\mathsf{1SDFE}
is adaptively single-key secure.

Proof of
Lemma

7.38
.

We construct an adversary
\mathpzc{B}
in the selective security game of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
as follows. To simulate the adaptive security game of
\mathsf{SDFE}
,
\mathpzc{B}
runs
\mathpzc{A}
attacking
\mathsf{1SDFE}
.
\mathpzc{A}
adaptively sends key queries
f_{1},\cdots,f_{q}
.
\mathpzc{B}
simulates the game of
\mathsf{SDFE}
as follows.

Setup:

\mathpzc{B}
receives a public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}
chooses
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and generates
\mathsf{K}
and
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
by using
\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
and
\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K},\tau^{\prime}_{i})
,
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}_{i})
, and the public key
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}\coloneqq i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime}_{i},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}},\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}])
for
\mathsf{SDFE}
according to
Figure

4
by using the given
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
.
\mathpzc{B}
sends
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

Key Generation:

When
f_{k}
(i.e.,
k
-th query) is queried,
\mathpzc{B}
checks
f_{k}(x_{0})=f_{k}(x_{1})
and outputs
\bot
if it does not hold. Otherwise,
\mathpzc{B}
checks whether
\tau^{\prime}_{i}=\tau_{k}
(i.e.,
i
-th tag in
[2^{\ell}]
is the same as the tag for
k
-th key query).
\mathpzc{B}
passes
f_{k}
to its challenger, receives
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k}}
, and sends
(\tau^{\prime}_{i},\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k}})
to
\mathpzc{A}
. If
\tau^{\prime}_{i}\neq\tau_{k}
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{msk}_{\tau_{k}},f_{k})
by using
(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau_{k}},\mathsf{msk}_{\tau_{k}})\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}}(\tau_{k}))
and returns it. Note that we do not need
\mathsf{msk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
for this simulation since
\mathpzc{B}
receives
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k}}
from its challenger if
\tau^{\prime}_{i}=\tau_{k}
.

Encryption:

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x_{0},x_{1})
to
\mathpzc{B}
,
\mathpzc{B}
passes
(x_{0},x_{1})
to its challenger and receives
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
. Then,
\mathpzc{B}
generates the challenge ciphertext
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}^{\ast}[\tau^{\prime}_{i},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{0},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}])
(obfuscated
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}

Figure

5
).

The simulation is completed. If
\mathpzc{B}
receives
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}=\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{0})
, it perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i}
. If
\mathpzc{B}
receives
\mathsf{ct}^{\ast}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}=\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{1})
, it perfectly simulates
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i}
. This completes the proof of the lemma.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}^{i}
:

We change
r_{i}
and
r^{\prime}_{i}
into
r_{i}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
and
r^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
. We can show
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{5}^{i}- \Adv_{4}^{i} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
for every
i\in[2^{\ell}]
by using the pseudorandomness at punctured point of puncturable PRF.

From the definition of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
,
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
, and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i}
, the behaviors of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}^{i}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i+1}
are the same. Thus,
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{1}^{i+1}- \Adv_{5}^{i} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
holds for every
i\in[2^{\ell}-1]
due to the indistinguishability of
i\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
i\mathcal{O}_{2}
.
It also holds that
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{0}(1)- \Adv_{5}^{2^\ell} }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
based on the security guarantee of
i\mathcal{O}_{1}
and
i\mathcal{O}_{2}
.

There are
O(2^{\ell})
hybrid games. However, if
{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
is sub-exponentially small for all transitions,
\absolutevalue{\Adv_{0}(0)- \Adv_{0}(1)}
is negligible.
Note that it is sufficient to set
\ell={\mathrm{polylog}}(\lambda)
.

Padding Parameter.

The proof of security relies on the indistinguishability of obfuscated
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
defined in
Figures

2
and
4
,
and that of obfuscated
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{\ast}
defined in
Figures

3
and
5
. Accordingly, we set
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{S}}\coloneqq\max(\absolutevalue{\sfS_{\sfonefe}},\absolutevalue{\sfS_{\sfonefe}^*})
and
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}\coloneqq\max(\absolutevalue{\sfE_{\sfonefe}},\absolutevalue{\sfE_{\sfonefe}^*})
.

The circuits
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{*}
compute a puncturable PRF over domain
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and a key pair of
\mathsf{1FE}
, and may have punctured PRF keys and a public key hardwired. The circuits
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{*}
run the circuit
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
and compute a puncturable PRF over domain
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and a ciphertext of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
, and may have punctured PRF keys, tags, plaintexts, and a hard-wired ciphertext. Note that
\ell
is a polynomial of
\lambda
. Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{S}}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda,n,s),

(50)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda,n,s,\absolutevalue{\pkhat}).

(51)

Therefore, we complete the proof of
Theorem

7.37
.

Theorem 7.39.

If
\mathsf{1SDFE}
is challenge-only strong
\gamma/2
-anti-piracy secure and sub-exponentially adaptively single-key secure,
i\mathcal{O}
is a sub-exponentially secure IO for
\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
, and
\mathsf{PPRF}
and
\mathsf{PPRF}^{\prime}
are sub-exponentially secure puncturable PRFs, then
\mathsf{SDFE}
is strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy secure.

Proof of
Theorem

7.39
.

Let
\mathpzc{A}
be an adversary attacking the strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy of
\mathsf{SDFE}
.
We define a sequence of hybrid games.
For the hybrid games, we define the following values.
Let
q
be the total number of key queries by
\mathpzc{A}
except for the challenge query
f^{\ast}
. Note that
q
could be any polynomial.
When we start the strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy security game, the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
is fixed, and
q
is also fixed. We choose tags
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
for
q
key queries and
\tau^{\ast}
for the challenge query at the beginning of the game.
We can interpret
\ell
bit strings as integers and assume that there is no
i,j
such that
i\neq j
and
\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}
without loss of generality.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
:

The first game is the original strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy experiment, that is,
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SDFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{strong}\mbox{-}\mathsf{anti}\mbox{-}\mathsf{piracy}}(1^{\lambda},\gamma)
.

1.

The challenger generates
(\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\widehat{\mathsf{msk}})=(i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}),\mathsf{K})\leftarrow\mathsf{SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends key queries
f_{k}
to the challenger and the challenger returns
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}_{f_{k}}=(\tau_{k},\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k},\tau_{k}})\leftarrow\mathsf{SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\widehat{\mathsf{msk}},f_{k})
where
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{k},\tau_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{msk}_{\tau_{k}},f_{k})
.

3.

At some point
\mathpzc{A}
sends a challenge query
f^{*}
to the challenger and the challenger returns
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}=(\tau^{\ast},\mathpzc{sk}_{f^{\ast}})\leftarrow\mathsf{SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\widehat{\mathsf{msk}},f^{\ast})
where
\mathpzc{sk}_{f^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{msk}_{\tau^{\ast}},f^{*})
.

4.

Again,
\mathpzc{A}
sends
f_{k}
to the challenger and the challenger returns
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}_{f_{k}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\widehat{\mathsf{msk}},f_{k})
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(x_{0},x_{1})
and two (possibly entangled) quantum decryptors
\mathpzc{D}_{1}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}],\boldsymbol{U}_{1})
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}],\boldsymbol{U}_{2})
, where
\forall i\ f_{i}(x_{0})=f_{i}(x_{1})
,
f^{*}(x_{0})\neq f^{*}(x_{1})
,
\mathpzc{q}
is a quantum state over registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}
, and
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
are general quantum circuits.

6.

For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{i,D}
be the following mixture of projective measurements acting on some quantum state
\mathpzc{q}^{\prime}
:

•

Sample a uniform
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
. Compute
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},x_{\mathsf{coin}})
and set
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
.

•

Run the quantum decryptor
(\mathpzc{q}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{i})
on input
\mathsf{ct}
. If the outcome is
\mathsf{coin}
, output
1
. Otherwise, output
0
.

Let
D
be the distribution over pairs
(\mathsf{coin},\mathsf{ct})
defined in the first item above, and let
\mathcal{E}_{i}=\{\boldsymbol{E}^{i}_{(\mathsf{coin},\mathsf{ct})}\}_{\mathsf{coin},\mathsf{ct}}
be a collection of projective measurements where
\boldsymbol{E}^{i}_{(\mathsf{coin},\mathsf{ct})}
is the projective measurement described in the second item above.

\mathcal{P}_{i,D}
is the mixture of projective measurements associated to
D
and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

7.

The challenger runs the test for a
\gamma
-good FE decryptor with respect to
\mathsf{pk}
,
(x_{0},x_{1})
, and
f^{\ast}
on
\mathpzc{D}_{1}
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}
.
The challenger outputs
1
if both tests pass, otherwise outputs
0
.

In the following hybrid games, we gradually change
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
for the
i
-th tag
\tau^{\prime}_{i}
, which is the
\ell
-bit string representation of
i\in[2^{\ell}]
, with the following exception.
Let
i^{\ast}\in[2^{\ell}]
is the integer representation of
\tau^{\ast}\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.
Instead of going over indices
\{1,\ldots,i^{\ast}-1,i^{\ast},i^{\ast}+1,\ldots,2^{\ell}\}
, we go over indices
I_{i^{\ast}}\coloneqq\{1,\ldots,i^{\ast}-1,i^{\ast}+1,\ldots,2^{\ell},i^{\ast}\}
. That is, we skip the tag
\tau_{i^{\ast}}
and go to
\tau_{i^{\ast}}
after we finish the
2^{\ell}
-th tag
\tau_{2^{\ell}}
.
It could happen that
\tau^{\prime}_{i}=\tau_{j}
for some
i\in[2^{\ell}]
and
j\in[q]
.
Let
\mathsf{Adv}_{x}^{i}
be the advantage of
\mathpzc{A}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{x}^{i}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i}
:

This is defined for
i\in[I_{i^{\ast}}]
and the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}^{i-1}
except that we generate
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
as obfuscated
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
described in
Figure

6
. Note that we define
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}^{0}=\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
. In this hybrid game, we set
r_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
,
\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K},\tau^{\prime}_{i})
, and
(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},\mathsf{msk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}})\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};r_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}})
.

The behavior of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is the same as that of
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
since the hard-wired
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{1}}
in
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is the same as the output of
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
on the input
\tau^{\prime}_{1}
. Their size is also the same since we pad circuit
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
to have the same size as
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{*}
. Then, we can use the indistinguishability guarantee of
i\mathcal{O}_{1}
and it holds that
i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\mathsf{K}])
is computationally indistinguishable from
i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}^{\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime}_{i},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}},\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}])
. Let
D^{(1,i)}
be the distribution over pairs
(\mathsf{coin},\mathsf{ct})
defined in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
except that
i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}^{\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\prime}_{i},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\prime}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}])
as
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
. Then,
D^{(1,1)}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D
.

:

. Set

\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i}
:

This is defined for
i\in[I_{i^{\ast}}]
and the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{2}^{i}
except that we use distribution
D^{(3,i)}
, which is the same as
D^{(2,i)}
except that setup randomness
r_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{Setup}}
and encryption randomness
r^{\prime}_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{Enc}}
are uniformly random. By the punctured pseudorandomness of
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}
,
D^{(3,i)}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D^{(2,i)}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i}
:

This is defined for
i\in[I_{i^{\ast}}]\setminus\{i^{\ast}\}
and the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i}
except that we use distribution
D^{(4,i)}
, which is the same as
D^{(3,i)}
except that the ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
is
\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{1})
instead of
\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}},x_{\mathsf{coin}})
.
In
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i}
, we do not need randomness to generate
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
.
We just hardwire
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
into
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{E}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
, respectively.
In addition, it holds that
f_{k}(x_{0})=f_{k}(x_{1})
for all
k\in[q]
by the requirement of strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy security. For every
i\in[I_{i^{\ast}}]\setminus\{i^{\ast}\}
, by the adaptive security of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
under the public key
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\prime}_{i}}
,
D^{(4,i)}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D^{(3,i)}
. Note that we do not need to generate a functional decryption key for
i\in[I_{i^{\ast}}]
such that
\tau^{\prime}_{i}\neq\tau_{k}
.
The detail of this indistinguishability is almost the same as
Lemma

7.38
, and we omit it.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}^{i}
:

This is defined for
i\in[I_{i^{\ast}}]\setminus\{i^{\ast}\}
and the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i}
except that we use distribution
D^{(5,i)}
, which is the same as
D^{(4,i)}
except that we use
r_{i}=\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
and
r^{\prime}_{i}=\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{K}^{\prime}}(\tau^{\prime}_{i})
.
By the punctured pseudorandomness of
\mathsf{F}
and
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}
,
D^{(5,i)}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D^{(4,i)}
.

We finish describing one cycle of our hybrid games.
We move from
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}^{i}
to
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i+1}
.
From the definition of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i}
, the behaviors of
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{5}^{i}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{1}^{i+1}
are the same.
It holds that
D^{(1,i+1)}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D^{(5,i)}
.
So, we can arrive at
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i^{\ast}}
by iterating the transitions so far.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i^{\ast}}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i^{\ast}}
except that we change the distribution
D^{(3,i^{\ast})}
over pairs
(\mathsf{coin},\mathsf{ct})
into

as follows.

•

Sample a uniform
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

•

Sample
r^{\prime}_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{Enc}}
and compute
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}},x_{\mathsf{coin}};r^{\prime}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda})
,
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}^{\prime}(\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\tau^{\ast})
, and
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\allowbreak\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}])
where
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is described in
Figure

8
. Set
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
.

Encryption Circuit
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}](\tau)

Hardwired:

tag
\tau^{\ast}
, public key
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
(this is an obfuscated circuit), puncturable PRF key
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}}
, plaintext
x_{1}
, and ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}
.

Input:

tag
\tau\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
.

Padding:

circuit is padded to size
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}\coloneqq\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}(\lambda,n,s)
, which is determined in analysis.

1.

If
\tau^{\ast}=\tau
, output
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}
.

2.

Else, compute

and the circuit
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
on input
\tau
, that is,
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}(\tau)
,

Output

Circuit
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}]

From the definitions of
\mathsf{E}^{\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
,
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
, and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{3}^{i^{\ast}}
, the behaviors of
\mathsf{E}^{\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
in

is the same as that of
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i^{\ast}}
.

\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}
. Then, we can use the indistinguishability of
i\mathcal{O}_{2}
, and it holds that

.
This change is for erasing information about
\mathsf{coin}
from
\mathsf{E}^{\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
.

\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
:

This is the same as
\mathsf{Hyb}_{4}^{i^{\ast}}
except that we choose PRF keys
\mathsf{K}_{1},\mathsf{K}_{2}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
at the beginning of the game and change the distribution
D^{(4,i^{\ast})}
over pairs
(\mathsf{coin},\mathsf{ct})
into
D^{(6)}
as follows.

•

Sample a uniform
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

•

Sample
r^{\prime}_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{Enc}}
, compute
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}\coloneqq\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}},x_{\mathsf{coin}};r^{\prime}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
r_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}^{(1)}_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}(\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
r_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}^{(2)}_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}(\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}^{\prime}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda};r_{1})
,
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}^{\prime}(\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\tau^{\ast})
, and
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\coloneqq i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\allowbreak\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}];r_{2})
where
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is described in
Figure

8
. Set
\mathsf{ct}\coloneqq\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
.

That is, we de-randomize generation of
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
. Note that the puncturing algorithm
\mathsf{Puncture}^{\prime}
is deterministic in the GGM construction based puncturable PRF
[
GGM86
,
BW13
,
KPTZ13
,
BGI14
]
.
By the pseudorandomness of
\mathsf{F}^{(1)}
and
\mathsf{F}^{(2)}
,
D^{(6)}
is computationally indistinguishable from
D^{(4,i^{\ast})}
.

There are
O(2^{\ell})
hybrid games between
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
and
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
. However, if all building blocks are sub-exponentially secure,
D^{(6)}
and
D
(the original distribution in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{0}
) are computationally indistinguishable since we can set
\ell={\mathrm{polylog}}(\lambda)
.
Note that we do not need the sub-exponential security for the challenge-only strong
\gamma/2
-anti-piracy security of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
since we do not use the anti-piracy security so far.

Thus, by
Theorem

7.10
, it holds that

\displaystyle\Tr[(\mathpzc{TI}_{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma-\epsilon}(\mathcal{P}_{1,D^{(6)}})\otimes\mathpzc{TI}_{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma-\epsilon}(\mathcal{P}_{2,D^{(6)}}))\mathpzc{q}]

(52)

\displaystyle\geq\Tr[(\mathpzc{TI}_{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma}(\mathcal{P}_{1,D})\otimes\mathpzc{TI}_{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma}(\mathcal{P}_{2,D}))\mathpzc{q}]-\delta,

(53)

where
\delta
is some negligible function.
That is, it holds that
\mathsf{Adv}_{6}\geq\mathsf{Adv}_{0}-{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

We prove the following.

Lemma 7.40.

If
\mathsf{1SDFE}
is challenge-only strong
\gamma/2
-anti-piracy secure, it holds that
\mathsf{Adv}_{6}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.40
.

Suppose that
\mathsf{Adv}_{6}
is non-negligible for a contradiction.
We construct a QPT algorithm
\mathpzc{B}
for the strong
\gamma/2
-anti-piracy game of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
by using the adversary
\mathpzc{A}
of the strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy game of
\mathsf{SDFE}
as follows.

1.

\mathpzc{B}
chooses
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{q},\tau^{\ast}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and
\mathsf{K}_{1},\mathsf{K}_{2}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
, and generates
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

\mathpzc{B}
generates
\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Puncture}(\mathsf{K},\tau^{\ast})
.

3.

\mathpzc{B}
receives
\mathsf{pk}^{\ast}
from its challenger, sets
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}}\coloneqq\mathsf{pk}^{\ast}
, and constructs
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},\allowbreak\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}}]
described in
Figure

6
by choosing
\tau^{\ast}
for the challenge query
f^{\ast}
.

\mathpzc{B}
sets
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}\coloneqq i\mathcal{O}_{1}(\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\mathsf{F}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}}])
and sends it to
\mathpzc{A}
.

4.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends a key query
f_{i}
,
\mathpzc{B}
generates
(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau_{i}},\mathsf{msk}_{\tau_{i}})\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda};\allowbreak\mathsf{PRF}_{\mathsf{K}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}}}(\tau_{i}))
and
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{i},\tau_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{msk}_{\tau_{i}},f_{i})
, sets
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}_{f_{i},\tau_{i}}\coloneqq(\tau_{i},\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{i},\tau_{i}})
, and sends
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}_{f_{i}}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

5.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends the challenge query
f^{\ast}
,
\mathpzc{B}
passes it to its challenger and receives
\mathpzc{sk}_{f^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{msk}^{\ast},f^{\ast})
, and sends
\widehat{\mathpzc{sk}}_{f^{\ast}}\coloneqq(\tau^{\ast},\mathpzc{sk}_{f^{\ast}})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

6.

Again,
\mathpzc{B}
can answer key queries
f_{i}
from
\mathpzc{A}
as the fourth item.

7.

At some point,
\mathpzc{B}
receives
(x_{0},x_{1})
and two (possibly entangled) quantum decryptors
\mathpzc{D}_{1}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}],\boldsymbol{U}_{1})
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}],\boldsymbol{U}_{2})
, where
f^{*}(x_{0})\neq f^{*}(x_{1})
, from
\mathpzc{A}
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
\mathcal{P}_{i,D^{(6)}}
be the following mixture of projective measurements acting on some quantum state
\mathpzc{q}^{\prime}
:

•

Sample a uniform
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
.

•

Sample
r^{\prime}_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{Enc}}
,
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}},x_{\mathsf{coin}};r^{\prime}_{\tau^{\ast}})
.

•

Generate
r_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}^{(1)}_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}(\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
r_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{F}^{(2)}_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}(\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda};r_{1})
, and
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}}=\mathsf{Puncture}^{\prime}(\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\tau^{\ast})
.

•

Generate
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\leftarrow i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}];r_{2})
where
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
is described in
Figure

8
.

•

Run the quantum decryptor
(\mathpzc{q}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{U}_{i})
on input
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
. If the outcome is
\mathsf{coin}
, output
1
. Otherwise, output
0
.

8.

\mathpzc{B}
sets quantum decryptors
\mathpzc{D}_{1}^{\ast}
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}^{\ast}
as follows.

In this item, we denote pure state
\ket{x}\bra{x}
by
\ket{x}
for ease of notation.
First,
\mathpzc{B}
constructs
(\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}],\boldsymbol{U}_{i}^{\ast})
such that

•

Set
\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]\coloneqq\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]\otimes\ket{0^{\abs{\cthat}}}\otimes\ket{\gtag^\ast, \pkhat, 0^\ell, x_1, \prfkey_1,\prfkey_2,0^{\abs{r_1}},0^{\abs{r_2}},0^{\ell}}\otimes\ket{\ct_{\gtag^\ast}}
. Note that
\ket{\ct_{\gtag^\ast}}
is the input for
(\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}],\boldsymbol{U}_{i}^{\ast})
.

•

Unitary
\boldsymbol{U}^{\ast}_{i}
acts on
\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]
and the result is

\mathpzc{q}^{\prime}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]\coloneqq\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]\otimes\ket{\cthat}\otimes\ket{\gtag^\ast, \pkhat, \prf^\prime_{\ne\gtag^\ast}, x_1,\prfkey_1,\prfkey_2,r_1,r_2,\prfkey^\prime}\otimes\ket{\ct_{\gtag^\ast}},

(54)

where
r_{1}\coloneqq\mathsf{F}^{(1)}_{\mathsf{K}_{1}}(\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
r_{2}\coloneqq\mathsf{F}^{(2)}_{\mathsf{K}_{2}}(\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}})
,
\mathsf{K}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{PRF}.\mathsf{Gen}^{\prime}(1^{\lambda};r_{1})
,
\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}}\coloneqq\mathsf{Puncture}^{\prime}(\mathsf{K}^{\prime},\tau^{\ast})
, and
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\coloneqq i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}];r_{2})
. That is,
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}^{\ast}
constructs
\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}]
described in
Figure

8
and generates
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}\coloneqq i\mathcal{O}_{2}(\mathsf{E}^{\ast\ast}_{\mathsf{1fe}}[\tau^{\ast},\widehat{\mathsf{pk}},\mathsf{F}^{\prime}_{\neq\tau^{\ast}},x_{1},\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}];r_{2})
. Note that
\mathpzc{B}
can construct it since
\mathpzc{B}
has
\tau^{\ast}
,
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
,
\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
\mathsf{K}_{2}
, and
x_{1}
.

It is easy to see that if we apply
(\boldsymbol{U}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{I})
to
\mathpzc{q}^{\prime}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]
, we obtain an output of
\mathpzc{D}_{i}
for input
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
.
Thus,
\mathpzc{B}
sets
\mathpzc{D}_{1}^{\ast}\coloneqq(\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}],(\boldsymbol{U}_{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{I})\boldsymbol{U}_{1}^{\ast})
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}^{\ast}\coloneqq(\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}],(\boldsymbol{U}_{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{I})\boldsymbol{U}_{2}^{\ast})
.

\mathpzc{B}
sends
(x_{0},x_{1})
and
\mathpzc{D}_{1}^{\ast}
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}^{\ast}
to its challenger.

The challenger of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
runs the test by using the following.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{i,D^{\prime}}
be the following mixture of projective measurements acting on some quantum state
\mathpzc{q}_{\mathsf{1SDFE}}
:

•

Sample a uniformly random
\mathsf{coin}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
and compute
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathsf{1SDFE}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}},\allowbreak x_{\mathsf{coin}})
.

•

Run
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{D}^{\ast}(\mathsf{ct})
. If
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}=\mathsf{coin}
, output
1
, otherwise output
0
.

By the construction of
\boldsymbol{U}_{i}^{\ast}
, quantum decryptor
\mathpzc{D}_{i}^{\ast}
takes a ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\tau^{\ast}}
under
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}}
as input, converts it into a ciphertext
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
of
\mathsf{SDFE}
, and apply the quantum decryptor
\mathpzc{D}_{i}
from
\mathpzc{A}
. The converted ciphertext
\widehat{\mathsf{ct}}
perfectly simulates a ciphertext in
\mathsf{Hyb}_{6}
if an input to
\mathpzc{D}_{i}^{\ast}
is a ciphertext under
\mathsf{pk}_{\tau^{\ast}}
.

We assumed that
\mathsf{Adv}_{6}
is non-negligible at the beginning of this proof.
That is, applying
\mathpzc{TI}_{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma-\epsilon}(\mathcal{P}_{i,D^{(6)}})
on
\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]
results in two outcomes
1
with non-negligible probability.
That is, it holds that

\displaystyle\Tr[\mathpzc{TI}_{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma-\epsilon}(\mathcal{P}_{1,D^{(6)}})\otimes\mathpzc{TI}_{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma-\epsilon}(\mathcal{P}_{2,D^{(6)}})\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}]>{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(55)

This means that for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathpzc{q}^{\ast}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{i}]
is a
(\gamma-\epsilon)
-good distinguisher with respect to ciphertexts generated according to
D^{(6)}
.

Thus, if
\mathpzc{D}_{i}
works as a
(\gamma-\epsilon)
-good quantum distinguisher for
\mathsf{SDFE}
,
\mathpzc{D}_{i}^{\ast}
works as a
(\gamma-\epsilon)
-good quantum distinguisher for
\mathsf{1SDFE}
, where
\epsilon=\frac{\gamma}{2}
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

7.40
.

By using the relationships among
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SDFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{}}
,
\mathsf{Adv}_{x}^{i}
for
x\in\{1,\cdots,5\}
and
i\in[I_{i^{\ast}}]
,
\mathsf{Adv}_{x}^{i^{\ast}}
for
x\in\{1,\cdots,4\}
, and
\mathsf{Adv}_{6}
that we show above, we obtain
\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SDFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{strong}\mbox{-}\mathsf{anti}\mbox{-}\mathsf{piracy}}(\lambda,\gamma)\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda)
.
We almost complete the proof of
Theorem

7.39
. Lastly, we need to set the padding parameters for IO security.

Padding Parameter.

The proof of security relies on the indistinguishability of obfuscated
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{S}^{*}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
defined in Figures
2
and
6
,
and that of obfuscated
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
,
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{\ast}
, and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{\ast\ast}
defined in
Figures

3
,
7
and
8
. Accordingly, we set
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{S}}\coloneqq\max(\absolutevalue{\sfS_{\sfonefe}},\absolutevalue{\sfS_{\sfonefe}^*})
and
\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}\coloneqq\max(\absolutevalue{\sfE_{\sfonefe}},\absolutevalue{\sfE_{\sfonefe}^*},\absolutevalue{\sfE_\sfonefe^{\ast\ast}})
.

The circuits
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
and
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{*}
compute a puncturable PRF over domain
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and a key pair of
\mathsf{1FE}
, and may have punctured PRF keys and a public key hardwired. The circuits
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}
,
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{*}
, and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{1fe}}^{\ast\ast}
run the circuit
\widehat{\mathsf{pk}}
and compute a puncturable PRF over domain
\{0,1\}^{\ell}
and a ciphertext of
\mathsf{1SDFE}
, and may have punctured PRF keys, tags, plaintexts, and a hard-wired ciphertext. Note that
\ell
is a polynomial of
\lambda
. Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{S}}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda,n,s),

(56)

\displaystyle\mathsf{pad}_{\mathsf{E}}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathrm{poly}}(\lambda,n,s,\absolutevalue{\pkhat}).

(57)

Therefore, we complete the proof of
Theorem

7.39
.

We obtain the following corollary from
Corollaries

7.34
,
7.37
and
7.39
.

Corollary 7.41.

Assuming the existence of sub-exponentially secure IO for
\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
, and the sub-exponential hardness of QLWE, there exists an SDFE scheme for
\mathsf{P}/\mathsf{poly}
that satisfies adaptive security (
Definition

7.28
) and strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy security for any inverse polynomial
\gamma
.

Appendix A
CPA-Style Anti-Piracy Security Notions

We introduce the CPA-style anti-piracy security for SDE.

Definition A.1 (Anti-Piracy Security, CPA-Style
[
CLLZ21
]
).

We consider the CPA-style anti-piracy game
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SDE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{anti}\mbox{-}\mathsf{piracy}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)
between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
below.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{sk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

2.

The challenger generates
\mathpzc{sk}\leftarrow\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{sk})
and sends
(\mathsf{pk},\mathpzc{sk})
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

3.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(m_{0},m_{1})
and two (possibly entangled) quantum decryptors
\mathpzc{D}_{1}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}],\boldsymbol{U}_{1})
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}],\boldsymbol{U}_{2})
, where
m_{0}\neq m_{1}
,
\absolutevalue{m_0}=\absolutevalue{m_1}
,
\mathpzc{q}
is a quantum state over registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}
, and
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
are general quantum circuits.

4.

The challenger chooses
\mathsf{coin}_{1},\mathsf{coin}_{2}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
, and generates
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m_{\mathsf{coin}_{1}})
and
\mathsf{ct}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},m_{\mathsf{coin}_{2}})
. The challenger runs
m_{i}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{D}_{i}(\mathsf{ct}_{i})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
If
m_{i}^{\prime}=m_{\mathsf{coin}_{i}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, the challenger outputs
1
, otherwise outputs
0
.

We say that
\mathsf{SDE}
is
\gamma
-anti-piracy secure if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, it satisfies that

\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SDE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{anti}\mbox{-}\mathsf{piracy}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)=1]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\gamma(\lambda)+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(58)

Theorem A.2 (
[
CLLZ21
]
).

If an SDE scheme satisfies strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy security, it also satisfies
\gamma
-anti-piracry security (in the CPA-style).

We can define a similar security notion for SDFE.

Definition A.3 (Anti-Piracy Security for FE, CPA-Style).

We consider the CPA-style anti-piracy game for FE
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SDFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{anti}\mbox{-}\mathsf{piracy}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)
between the challenger and an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
below.

1.

The challenger generates
(\mathsf{pk},\mathsf{msk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and sends
\mathsf{pk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
sends key queries
f_{i}
to the challenger and receives
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{i}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}})
where
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f_{i})
.

3.

At some point
\mathpzc{A}
sends a challenge query
f^{*}
to the challenger and receives
\mathpzc{sk}_{f^{*}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{msk},f^{\ast})
.

4.

Again,
\mathpzc{A}
sends
f_{i}
to the challenger and receives
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{i}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{QKG}(\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}})
where
\mathsf{sk}_{f_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f_{i})
.

5.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs
(x_{0},x_{1})
and two (possibly entangled) quantum decryptors
\mathpzc{D}_{1}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}],\boldsymbol{U}_{1})
and
\mathpzc{D}_{2}=(\mathpzc{q}[{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}],\boldsymbol{U}_{2})
, where
\forall i\ f_{i}(x_{0})=f_{i}(x_{1})
,
f^{*}(x_{0})\neq f^{*}(x_{1})
,
\mathpzc{q}
is a quantum state over registers
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}{\mathsf{R}}}_{2}
, and
\boldsymbol{U}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{U}_{2}
are general quantum circuits.

6.

The challenger chooses
\mathsf{coin}_{1},\mathsf{coin}_{2}\leftarrow\{0,1\}
, and generates
\mathsf{ct}_{1}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x_{\mathsf{coin}_{1}})
and
\mathsf{ct}_{2}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk},x_{\mathsf{coin}_{2}})
. The challenger runs
\mathsf{coin}_{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathpzc{D}_{k}(\mathsf{ct}_{k})
for
k\in\{1,2\}
.
If
\mathsf{coin}_{k}^{\prime}=\mathsf{coin}_{k}
for
k\in\{1,2\}
, the challenger outputs
1
, otherwise outputs
0
.

We say that
\mathsf{SDFE}
is
\gamma
-anti-piracy secure if for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, it satisfies that

\Pr[\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{SDFE},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{anti}\mbox{-}\mathsf{piracy}\mbox{-}\mathsf{cpa}}(\lambda)=1]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\gamma(\lambda)+{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(59)

If
\mathpzc{A}
can send only the challenge query
f^{\ast}
during the experiment, we say that
\mathsf{SDFE}
is challenge-only
\gamma
-anti-piracy secure.

We can also define the CPA-style anti-piracy for the predictor-based quantum decryptors as
Definition

A.1
. That is,
\mathpzc{D}_{i}
outputs
f^{\ast}(x_{\mathsf{coin}_{i}})
for
i\in\{1,2\}
instead of
\mathsf{coin}_{i}
. We omit the variation.

Theorem A.4.

If an SDFE scheme satisfies strong
\gamma
-anti-piracy security, it also satisfies
\gamma
-anti-piracy security in the CPA-style.

The proof of this theorem is almost the same as that of
Theorem

A.2
, so we omit it to avoid replication.

Appendix B
Adaptive and Simulation-based Security for Secure Key Leasing

This section presents how to upgrade FE with secure key leasing security.

B.1
Adaptive Security for Secure Key Leasing

Ananth et al.
[
ABSV15
]
present transformations from selectively secure FE into adaptively secure FE. Almost the same transformation works for FE with secure key leasing.
The adaptive lessor security for SKFE with SKL is almost the same as
Definition

3.2
except that
\mathpzc{A}
declares
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
when
\mathpzc{A}
sends
\mathsf{cert}
to the challenger and we separate the key oracle into two types. The formal definition is as follows.

Definition B.1 (Adaptive Lessor Security).

We say that
\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
is an adaptively lessor secure SKFE-SKL scheme for
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
, and
\mathcal{F}
, if it satisfies the following requirement, formalized from the experiment
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathpzc{A},\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{lessor}}(1^{\lambda},\mathsf{coin})
between an adversary
\mathpzc{A}
and a challenger:

1.

At the beginning,
\mathpzc{A}
sends
1^{q+q^{\ast}}
to the challenger. The challenger runs
\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda},1^{q+q^{\ast}})
.
Throughout the experiment,
\mathpzc{A}
can access the following oracles.

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Enc}}}(x)
:

Given
x
, it returns
\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x)
.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}(f,1^{n})
:

Given
(f,1^{n})
, it generates
(\mathpzc{fsk},\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f,1^{n})
, and sends
\mathpzc{fsk}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{A}
can access this oracle at most
q
times.

2.

\mathpzc{A}
can access the following oracles before
\mathpzc{A}
declares the challenge plaintexts.

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}})
:

Given
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}})
, it generates
(\mathpzc{fsk}^{\ast},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast})\leftarrow\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}})
, adds
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
, and sends
\mathpzc{fsk}^{\ast}
to
\mathpzc{A}
.
\mathpzc{A}
can access this oracle at most
q^{\ast}
times.

O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(f^{\ast},\mathsf{cert}^{\ast})
:

Given
(f^{\ast},\mathsf{cert}^{\ast})
, it finds an entry
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},M)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. (If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
.) If
\top=\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\mathsf{cert}^{\ast})
and the number of queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Enc}}}
at this point is less than
n^{\ast}
, it returns
\top
and updates the entry into
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\top)
. Otherwise, it returns
\bot
.

3.

When
\mathpzc{A}
sends
(x_{0}^{\ast},x_{1}^{\ast})
to receive the challenge ciphertext, if there exists
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\bot)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
, or
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
received
(f,1^{n})
such that
f(x_{0}^{\ast})\neq f(x_{1}^{\ast})
, the challenger outputs
0
as the final output of this experiment. Otherwise, the challenger generates
\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x_{\mathsf{coin}}^{*})
and sends
\mathsf{ct}^{*}
to
\mathpzc{A}
. Hereafter,
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
rejects a query
(f,1^{n})
such that
f(x_{0}^{\ast})\neq f(x_{1}^{\ast})
and
\mathpzc{A}
cannot access
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}
.

4.

\mathpzc{A}
outputs a guess
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{coin}
. The challenger outputs
\mathsf{coin}^{\prime}
as the final output of the experiment.

For any QPT
\mathpzc{A}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Adv}_{\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A}}^{\mathsf{ada}\mbox{-}\mathsf{lessor}}(\lambda)\coloneqq\absolutevalue{\Pr[\expb{\SKFESKL,\qA}{ada}{lessor} (1^\secp,0) \out 1] - \Pr[\expb{\SKFESKL,\qA}{ada}{lessor} (1^\secp,1) \out 1] }\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(60)

Remark
B.2.

The definition above considers two key oracles
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}
unlike
Definition

3.2
to distinguish a functional decryption key which is deleted from one which is not deleted. The oracle
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}
accepts a query
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}})
such that
f^{\ast}(x_{0}^{\ast})\neq f^{\ast}(x_{1}^{\ast})
. We need to distinguish these two types of queries since we need to know whether
f(x_{0}^{\ast})=f(x_{1}^{\ast})
or not when an adversary sends a query in the security proof of the construction below. It is not clear whether we can achieve a stronger adaptive security where adversaries access a single key generation oracle as
Definition

3.2
. We leave it as an open question.

We construct an adaptively secure SKFE scheme with secure key leasing
\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL}=(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Cert},\mathsf{Vrfy})
using the following tools:

•

A selectively secure SKFE scheme with secure key leasing
\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{SKFE}=\mathsf{Sel}.(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Cert},\mathsf{Vrfy})
.

•

An adaptively single-ciphertext secure SKFE scheme
\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{SKFE}=\mathsf{1CT}.(\mathsf{Setup},\allowbreak\mathsf{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathsf{Dec})
.

•

A pseudorandom-secure SKE scheme
\mathsf{SKE}=\mathsf{SKE}.(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{D})
.

•

A PRF
\mathsf{F}
.

Let
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
be the length of
\mathsf{SKE}
ciphertexts and the length of random tags, respectively.
The definition of adaptive lessor security is a natural adaptive variant of
Definition

3.2
.
The description of
\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
is as follows.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

Generate
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{msk}\coloneqq\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk}
.

\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f,1^{n})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{msk}
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{1}}
and choose
\tau\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}}
.

•

Compute
(\mathsf{sel}.\mathpzc{sk}_{G},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sel}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},G[f,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau],1^{n})
, where the circuit
G
is described in
Figure

9
.

•

Output
\mathpzc{sk}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{sel}.\mathpzc{sk}_{G}
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x)
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{msk}
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and choose
\mathsf{K}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk},x)
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{K},0^{\lambda},0))
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}_{x}\coloneqq(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{sk}_{f},\mathsf{ct}_{x})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sel}.\mathpzc{sk}_{G}\leftarrow\mathpzc{sk}_{f}
and
(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{ct},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct})\leftarrow\mathsf{ct}_{x}
.

•

Compute
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sel}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathpzc{sk}_{G},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{ct})
.

•

Output
y\leftarrow\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{Dec}(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f},\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathpzc{Cert}(\mathpzc{sk}_{f})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sel}.\mathpzc{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathpzc{sk}_{f}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sel}.\mathpzc{Cert}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathpzc{sk}_{f})
.

\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{cert}=\mathsf{cert}
.

•

Output
\top/\bot\leftarrow\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{cert})
.

Key Generation Circuit
G[f,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau](\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{K},K_{\mathsf{ske}},\beta)

Hardwired:

function
f
, SKE ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}}
, tag
\tau
.

Input:

master secret key
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk}
, PRF key
\mathsf{K}
, SKE secret key
K_{\mathsf{ske}}
, bit
\beta
.

1.

If
\beta=0
, output
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk},f;\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau))
.

2.

Else, output
\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{D}(K_{\mathsf{ske}},\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}})
.

Figure 9:
Description of
G[f,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau]
.

We emphasize that
\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{SKFE}
is a classical standard SKFE scheme, and
\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{KG}
is a classical algorithm.
This does not spoil the lessor security of
\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{SKFE}
because
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
generated by the circuit
G
depends on
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk}
and
\mathsf{K}
from the encryption algorithm and
\tau
from the key generation algorithm.
At the decryption phase, we can obtain
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
under
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk}
from
\mathsf{Sel}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},G,1^{n})
and

(\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk},x),\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk},\mathsf{K},0^{\lambda},0))).

(61)

We can keep a copy of
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
. However, it does not help us to decrypt another ciphertext

(\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk}^{\prime},x^{\prime}),\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk}^{\prime},\mathsf{K}^{\prime},0^{\lambda},0))).

(62)

Note that
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk}
is freshly generated at the encryption phase.
That is, a functional decryption key
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}
is specific to only one pair of
\mathpzc{sk}_{f}
and
\mathsf{ct}_{x}

Thus, after we securely delete
\mathpzc{sk}_{f}
by using the deletion algorithm
\mathsf{Sel}.\mathpzc{Cert}
of
\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{SKFE}
, we can no longer decrypt a fresh ciphertext generated after the deletion. Since the deletion power is preserved, we can upgrade the selective security of
\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{SKFE}
to adaptive security by leveraging the adaptive security of
\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{SKFE}
as the transformation by Ananth et al.
[
ABSV15
]
.

Theorem B.3.

If
\mathsf{Sel}.\mathsf{SKFE}
is selectively lessor secure,
\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{SKFE}
is adaptively single-ciphertext secure and collusion-resistant,
\mathsf{SKE}
is pseudorandom-secure, and
\mathsf{F}
is a pseudorandom function,
\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
above is adaptively lessor secure.

Since the proof is almost the same as that by Ananth et al.
[
ABSV15
, Theorem 4 in the eprint ver.]
, we omit it to avoid the replication.
Note that the reduction needs to embed
\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathsf{1CT}.\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{1ct}.\mathsf{msk},f;\mathsf{F}_{\mathsf{K}}(\tau))
into
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}}
for a key query such that
f(x_{0}^{\ast})=f(x_{1}^{\ast})
, but
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}}
should be a junk ciphertext for a key query such that
f(x_{0}^{\ast})\neq f(x_{1}^{\ast})
.
The reduction knows which simulation-way is correct since
Definition

B.1
distinguishes two types of queries.

B.2
Simulation-based Security for Secure Key Leasing

Although we present indistinguishability-based security definitions for SKFE with secure key leasing in
Section

3
, we can also consider simulation-based security definitions for SKFE with secure key leasing.
We present the definition of adaptive lessor simulation-security.

Definition B.4 (Adaptive Lessor Simulation-security).

Let
\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
be an SKFE scheme with secure key leasing.
For a stateful QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}=(\mathpzc{A}_{1},\mathpzc{A}_{2},\mathpzc{A}_{3})
and a stateful QPT Simulator
\mathpzc{S}=(\mathpzc{SEnc},\mathpzc{SKG})
, we consider the two experiments described in
Figure

10
,
where

•

\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make total at most
q
queries to
\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\cdot)
(resp.
\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\cdot)
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{SKG}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
) in
\mathsf{Real}^{\mathsf{ad}\textrm{-}\mathsf{sim}}_{\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A}}(\lambda)
(resp.
\mathsf{Sim}^{\mathsf{ad}\textrm{-}\mathsf{sim}}_{\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{S}}(\lambda)
),

•

\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make total at most
q^{\ast}
queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}(\cdot,\cdot)
, which is the same as
\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\cdot)
except that it also adds
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\bot)
to
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
when it is invoked,

•

\mathpzc{A}
is allowed to make queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)
, which is given
(f^{\ast},\mathsf{cert}^{\ast})
, it finds an entry
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},M)
from
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
, and does the following: If there is no such entry, it returns
\bot
. If
\top=\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\mathsf{cert}^{\ast})
and the number of queries to
O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Enc}}}
at this point is less than
n^{\ast}
, it returns
\top
and updates the entry into
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\top)
. Otherwise, it returns
\bot
,

•

O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{SKG}}}(f,1^{n})=\mathpzc{SKG}(\mathsf{st},f,1^{n},f(x^{*}))
.

\mathsf{Real}^{\mathsf{ad}\textrm{-}\mathsf{sim}}_{\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A}}(\lambda)

\mathsf{Sim}^{\mathsf{ad}\textrm{-}\mathsf{sim}}_{\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL},\mathpzc{A},\mathpzc{S}}(\lambda)

1.

1^{q+q^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda})

1.

1^{q+q^{\ast}}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{1}(1^{\lambda})

2.

\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda},1^{q+q^{\ast}})

2.

\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda},1^{q+q^{\ast}})

3.

x^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{2}^{\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\cdot),O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}(\cdot,\cdot),O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}

3.

x^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{2}^{\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\cdot),O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}(\cdot,\cdot),O_{\mathtt{\mathsf{Vrfy}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}

4.

Output
0
if there exists
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\bot)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. Otherwise, go to the next step.

4.

Output
0
if there exists
(f^{\ast},1^{n^{\ast}},\mathsf{vk}^{\ast},\bot)
in
L_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}
. Otherwise, go to the next step.

5.

5.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the query/answer list for
\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot)

6.

6.

Let
(f_{i},1^{n_{i}})_{i\in[q]}
be the queries for
\mathsf{KG}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\cdot)

7.

7.

y_{i}:=f_{i}(x^{*})
for every
i\in[q]

8.

\mathsf{ct}^{*}\leftarrow\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x^{*})

8.

(\mathsf{ct}^{*},\mathsf{st})\leftarrow\mathpzc{SEnc}(\mathsf{msk},\mathcal{Q},(f_{i},1^{n_{i}},y_{i})_{i\in[q]})

9.

Output
b\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{3}(\mathsf{ct}^{*})^{\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot,\cdot)}

9.

Output
b\leftarrow\mathpzc{A}_{3}(\mathsf{ct}^{*})^{\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},\cdot),O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{SKG}}}(\cdot,\cdot)}

Figure 10
:
Security experiments for adaptively lessor simulation-secure SKFE-SKL

We say that
\mathsf{SKFE\textrm{-}SKL}
is adaptively lessor simulation-secure if there exists a QPT simulator
\mathpzc{S}
such that for any QPT adversary
\mathpzc{A}
, it satisfies that

\absolutevalue{\Pr[\sfreal{ad}{sim}_{\SKFESKL,\qA}(\sep)=1] - \Pr[\sfsim{ad}{sim}_{\SKFESKL,\qA,\qS}(\sep)=1]}\leq{\mathsf{negl}}(\lambda).

(63)

This is a natural simulation-based variant of adaptive lessor (indistinguishability-)security.
The simulator
\mathpzc{S}=(\mathpzc{SEnc},\mathpzc{SKG})
does not have
f^{\ast}_{j}(x^{\ast})
, and the simulated ciphertext does not reveal information about
f^{\ast}_{j}(x^{\ast})
.

Remark
B.5.

We consider two key oracles
\mathpzc{KG}
and
O_{\mathtt{\mathpzc{KG}}}^{\ast}
as
Definition

B.1
to distinguish two types of queries.
We can achieve this simulation-based security since we use adaptively lessor secure SKFE-SKL in the sense of
Definition

B.1
as a building block below. It is also an open question to achieve a stronger adaptive simulation-based security where adversaries access a single key generation oracle as
Definition

3.2
.

De Caro, Iovino, Jani, O’Neil, Paneth, and Persiano
[
DIJ+13
]
present a transformation from indistinguishability-based secure FE to simulation-based secure FE. The transformation works if the number of key queries before a challenge ciphertext is given is a-priori bounded.
We can apply almost the same transformation to SKFE with secure leasing since we modify a plaintext in the ciphertext and a function embedded in a functional decryption key.

We construct an adaptively lessor simulation-secure SKFE scheme with secure key leasing
\mathsf{adaSKL}=\mathsf{adaSKL}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Cert},\mathsf{Vrfy})
using the following tools:

•

An adaptively lessor (indistinguishability-)secure SKFE scheme with secure key leasing
\mathsf{indSKL}=\mathsf{indSKL}.(\mathsf{Setup},\mathpzc{KG},\mathsf{Enc},\allowbreak\mathpzc{Dec},\mathpzc{Cert},\mathsf{Vrfy})
.

•

A pseudorandom-secure SKE scheme
\mathsf{SKE}=\mathsf{SKE}.(\mathsf{E},\mathsf{D})
.

Let
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
, and
m
be the length of
\mathsf{SKE}
ciphertexts, the length of random tags, and the output length, respectively.
The description of
\mathsf{adaSKL}
is as follows.

\mathsf{adaSKL}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
:

•

Generate
\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{msk}\coloneqq\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{msk}
.

\mathsf{adaSKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{msk},f,1^{n})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{msk}
.

•

Generate
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{1}}
and choose
\tau\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}}
.

•

Compute
(\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{T},\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},T[f,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau],1^{n})
, where the circuit
T
is described in
Figure

11
.

•

Output
\mathpzc{sk}_{f}\coloneqq\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{T}
and
\mathsf{vk}\coloneqq\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{vk}
.

\mathsf{adaSKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{msk},x)
:

•

Parse
(\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{msk},K_{\mathsf{ske}})\leftarrow\mathsf{msk}
.

•

Set
x^{\prime}\coloneqq(x,0^{\lambda},(0^{\ell_{2}},0^{m}),\ldots,(0^{\ell_{2}},0^{m}),0)
, where
m
is the output length of functions.

•

Generate
\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{msk},x^{\prime})
.

•

Output
\mathsf{ct}_{x}\coloneqq\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{ct}
.

\mathsf{adaSKL}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathpzc{sk}_{f},\mathsf{ct}_{x})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{T}\leftarrow\mathpzc{sk}_{f}
and
\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{ct}\leftarrow\mathsf{ct}_{x}
.

•

Output
y\leftarrow\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathpzc{Dec}(\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{T},\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{ct})
.

\mathsf{adaSKL}.\mathpzc{Cert}(\mathpzc{sk}_{f})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{f}\leftarrow\mathpzc{sk}_{f}
.

•

Output
\mathsf{cert}\coloneqq\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{cert}\leftarrow\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathpzc{Cert}(\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{f})
.

\mathsf{adaSKL}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{cert})
:

•

Parse
\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{vk}=\mathsf{vk}
and
\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{cert}=\mathsf{cert}
.

•

Output
\top/\bot\leftarrow\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathsf{Vrfy}(\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{vk},\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{cert})
.

Trapdoor Circuit
T[f,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau](x,K_{\mathsf{ske}},(\tau_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(\tau_{q},y_{q}),\beta)

Hardwired:

function
f
, SKE ciphertext
\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}}
, tag
\tau
.

Input:

plaintext
x
, SKE secret key
K_{\mathsf{ske}}
,
q
pairs of tag and output
(\tau_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(\tau_{q},y_{q})
, bit
\beta
.

1.

If
\beta=1
, do the following

•

If there exists
i
such that
\tau=\tau_{i}
, output
y_{i}
.

•

Else output
y^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{D}(K_{\mathsf{ske}},\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}})
.

2.

Else, output
f(x)
.

Figure 11:
Description of
T[f,\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske}},\tau]
.

As we see above, we expand the plaintext space and use the trapdoor circuit
T
as De Caro et al.
[
DIJ+13
]
.
We can construct a QPT simulator as follows.

1.

Generates
\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{msk}\leftarrow\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\lambda})
and
K_{\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\lambda}
.

2.

Receives
\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{i}}=(\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{f_{i}},\mathsf{ct}_{\mathsf{ske},i},\tau_{i})
for
i\in[q_{\mathsf{pre}}]
and outputs for pre-challenge key queries, that is,
f_{1}(x^{\ast}),\ldots,f_{q_{\mathsf{pre}}}(x^{\ast})
.

3.

Generates a challenge ciphertext
\mathsf{indSKL}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{msk},(0,K_{\mathsf{ske}},(\tau_{1},f_{1}(x^{\ast})),\ldots,\allowbreak(\tau_{q_{\mathsf{pre}}},f_{q_{\mathsf{pre}}}(x^{\ast})),1))
.

4.

Simulates functional decryption keys for post-challenge key queries as follows. When an output value
f^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\ast})
for a key query
f^{\prime}_{i}
is given, generates
(\mathsf{ind}.\mathpzc{sk}_{T},\mathsf{ind}.\mathsf{vk})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sel}.\mathpzc{KG}(\mathsf{sel}.\mathsf{msk},T[f^{\prime}_{i},\mathsf{ct}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{ske},i},\tau^{\prime}_{i}],1^{n})
where
\mathsf{ct}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{ske},i}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{E}(K_{\mathsf{ske}},f^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\ast}))
and
\tau^{\prime}_{i}\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{\ell_{2}}
.

Note that the simulator should not have
f^{\ast}_{j}(x^{\ast})
for any
j\in[q^{\ast}]
since
\{\mathpzc{sk}_{f^{\ast}_{j}}\}_{j\in[q^{\ast}]}
are deleted and the challenge ciphertext should not reveal information about
\{f^{\ast}_{j}(x^{\ast})\}_{j\in[q^{\ast}]}
, where
\{f^{\ast}_{j}\}_{j\in[q^{\ast}]}
are the challenge functions, and the simulation-security is satisfied.

Since the flag
\beta=1
in the simulated challenge ciphertext, the trapdoor circuit
T
works at the trapdoor branch.
For functional decryption keys before the challenge ciphertext,
T
works at the first item in the
\beta=1
branch since
\tau_{i}
is a tag embedded in the functional decryption keys for pre-challenge key queries. So, the output of
T
is an embedded
f_{i}(x^{\ast})
. This is consistent.
For functional decryption keys after the challenge ciphertext,
T
works at the second item in the
\beta=1
branch since
\tau^{\prime}_{i}
is uniformly random and must be different from
\tau_{i}
except negligible probability. In addition,
y^{\prime}=f^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\ast})
since
\mathsf{ct}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{ske}}\leftarrow\mathsf{SKE}.\mathsf{E}(K_{\mathsf{ske}},f^{\prime}_{i}(x^{\ast}))
. This is consistent. Thus, the simulated ciphertext does not include information about
\{f^{\ast}_{j}(x^{\ast})\}_{j\in[q^{\ast}]}
.

In reducing to the adaptive lessor (indistinguishability)-security, the simulator can simulate the key generation and encryption oracles in the simulation-based game by using the oracles in the indistinguishability-based game.

Theorem B.6.

If
\mathsf{indSKL}
is adaptively lessor (indistinguishability-)secure and
\mathsf{SKE}
is pseuodrandom-secure,
\mathsf{adaSKL}
is adaptively lessor simulation-secure.

Since the proof is almost the same as that by De Caro et al.
[
DIJ+13
, Theorem 14 in the eprint ver.]
, we omit it to avoid the replication.
